</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC991" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="991" style="position:relative"><span class="pl-c1">\textbf</span>{<span class="pl-mb">FIGURE<span class="pl-cce">\_</span>11-5</span>}.</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC992" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="992" style="position:relative"><span class="pl-c1">\href</span>{../figures/FIGURE_11-5.png}{} Blow-up</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC993" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="993" style="position:relative">of previous figure showing slight divergence at smaller wind energies.</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC994" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="994" style="position:relative">

The</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC998" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="998" style="position:relative">longer it takes to reach a certain level, the greater the likelihood</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC999" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="999" style="position:relative">that the curve will match the predicted levels.</div></div></div><div class="react-code-text react-code-line-contents" style="min-height:auto"><div><div id="LC1000" class="react-file-line html-div" data-testid="code-cell" data-line-number="1000" style="position:relative">

fontspec","  \\defaultfontfeatures{Scale=MatchLowercase}","  \\defaultfontfeatures[\\rmfamily]{Ligatures=TeX,Scale=1}","\\fi","\\usepackage{lmodern}","\\ifPDFTeX\\else","  % xetex/luatex font available","  \\usepackage[]{microtype}","  \\UseMicrotypeSet[protrusion]{basicmath} % disable protrusion for tt fonts","}{}","\\makeatletter","\\@ifundefined{KOMAClassName}{% if non-KOMA class"," head/foot","\\IfFileExists{footnotehyper.sty}{\\usepackage{footnotehyper}}{\\usepackage{footnote}}","\\makesavenoteenv{longtable}","\\setlength{\\emergencystretch}{3em} % prevent overfull lines","\\providecommand{\\tightlist}{%","  \\setlength{\\itemsep}{0pt}\\setlength{\\parskip}{0pt}}","\\setcounter{secnumdepth}{-\\maxdimen} % remove section numbering","\\ifLuaTeX","  \\usepackage{selnolig}  % disable illegal ligatures","\\fi","\\IfFileExists{bookmark.sty}{\\usepackage{bookmark}}{\\usepackage{hyperref}}","\\IfFileExists{xurl.sty}{\\usepackage{xurl}}{} % add URL line breaks if available","\\urlstyle{same}","\\hypersetup{","  hidelinks,","  pdfcreator={LaTeX via pandoc}}","","\\author{}","\\date{}","","\\begin{document}","","\\hypertarget{wind-energy}{%","\\subsection{Wind Energy}\\label{wind-energy}}","","\\begin{quote}","Wind energy arises from the atmosphere's forced and natural response to","external stimulus","\\end{quote}","","\\hypertarget{deterministic-wind---qbo}{%","\\subsubsection*{Deterministic Wind -","QBO}\\label{deterministic-wind---qbo}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Deterministic Wind - QBO}","","The Quasi-Biennial Oscillation (QBO) in equatorial stratospheric winds","is an approximately 28-month period of east-west reversal in wind","direction {[}1{]}. The QBO is considered an important indicator for","natural climate variability such as monsoons and in global circulation","patterns that reach to the polar vortex. However, it's ``spectacular''","{[}2{]} behavior is still not well understood as it takes much tuning to","make it emerge from large general circulation models (GCM) {[}3{]} of","the climate {[}4{]}--{[}8{]}. For example, in one study {[}8{]},","simulation showed that the emergent QBO period was inversely","proportional to a gravity wave momentum flux forced pressure.","","In keeping with the well-known lunisolar orbital forcing of oceanic","tides, it was long postulated that the QBO might be similarly stimulated","by orbital tidal forces, or with annual cycle {[}9{]}. Richard Lindzen","(who did the original research on QBO{[}10{]}) suggested that ``\\emph{it","is relatively easy to isolate tidal phenomena in the data}'' and that","``\\emph{Lunar tides are especially well suited to such studies since it","is unlikely that lunar periods could be produced by anything other than","the lunar tidal potential}'' {[}11{]}. He earlier stated \\emph{``For","oscillations of tidal periods, the nature of the forcing is clear''}","{[}12{]}. However, no match to the 28-month period has been made to any","known lunar or solar cycle and so the effect was thought to have a weak","amplitude. Yet, this absence of reported evidence for a lunisolar","connection in the research literature is not an indicator that it","doesn't exist. Instead, we may need to look more closely at the","fundamentals (see this for example {[}13{]}, {[}14{]}) or make sure that","these factors are not inadvertently filtered out {[}15{]}.","","We start with the fact that the QBO is a standing wave pattern with a","wavenumber of zero, which suggests that it could be a solution to a wave","equation. The candidate wave equation that is initially considered for","any fluid flow (atmospheric or oceanic) is the full Navier-Stokes","equations in the form of the \\emph{primitive equations}, and when these","can be simplified, in the form known as Laplace's Tidal Equations","{[}16{]}.","","For a fluid sheet of average thickness \\emph{D}, the vertical tidal","elevation \\emph{ζ}~, as well as the horizontal velocity components","\\emph{u}~ and \\emph{v}~ (in the latitude \\emph{φ}~ and longitude","\\emph{λ}~ directions), this is the set of Laplace\\textquotesingle s","tidal equations.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8949}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1051}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{a\\cos(\\varphi)}\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(uD) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(vD\\cos(\\varphi))\\rbrack = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial t} - v(2\\Omega sin(\\varphi)) + \\frac{1}{a\\cos(\\varphi)}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(g\\zeta + U) = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} + u(2\\Omega sin(\\varphi)) + \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U) = 0,","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-1","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where Ω~ is the angular frequency of the planet\\textquotesingle s","rotation, \\emph{g}~ is the planet\\textquotesingle s gravitational","acceleration at the mean ocean surface, \\emph{a}~ is the planetary","radius, and \\emph{U}~ is the external gravitational tidal-forcing","potential. The goal is that along the equator, for \\emph{φ}~at zero, we","can reduce these three equations into one.","","As we will re-derive a simplification of these equations in the next","chapter when we discuss ENSO, it is enough at the present to point to a","simplifying differential relation below:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial\\varphi} = \\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial t}\\frac{\\partial t}{\\partial\\varphi}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-2","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Via this and the other simplifying assumption of the Coriolis forces","cancelling at the equator, we obtain the following potentially highly","nonlinear result:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\zeta(t) = \\sin(\\sqrt{A}\\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\sin(\\omega_{i}t) + \\theta_{0})}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-3","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This is subject to fixing \\emph{φ}(\\emph{t})~to a periodic function with","a long-term mean of zero","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\varphi}{\\partial t} = \\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\omega_{i}\\cos(\\omega_{i}t)}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-4","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This essentially serves as a latitudinal tractive force that acts as a","perturbation across the equatorial boundary, forcing a perpendicular","curl onto the longitudinal motion of the QBO winds. This could be","considered an artifact of the Coriolis effect or angular Doppler effect","where the force can vary along the equatorial line, reminiscent of the","lunar-forced modulation of the ionosphere's equatorial electrojet which","circumnavigates the Earth's (magnetic) dip equator {[}17{]}--{[}19{]}","and perhaps analogous to Berry curvature of a topological insulator","{[}20{]}, {[}21{]}. In fact, the model of topological insulators as","applied to equatorial phenomena such as QBO and ENSO will give rise to","Rossby, Kelvin, and Yanai waves {[}152{]}.","","Now consider that the QBO itself is precisely the","∂\\emph{v/}∂\\emph{t}~~term - the horizontal longitudinal acceleration of","the fluid, i.e. leading to the observed characteristic waveform - which","can be derived from the above by applying the solution to","Laplace\\textquotesingle s third tidal equation in simplified form:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} = \\cos(\\sqrt{A}\\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\sin(\\omega_{i}t)} + \\theta_{0})\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-5","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Note again that this is the QBO acceleration and not the QBO velocity,","which is usually reported.","","\\textbf{Harmonics}","","The form of the last equation suppresses large amplitudes, thus leading","to the formation of harmonics of the fundamental frequencies","\\(\\omega_{i}\\). One potential candidate for \\(\\omega_{i}\\) is to apply a","strong annual pulse to one of the known lunar monthly periods, such as","the lunar or draconic cycle of 27.212 days. This will generate the","physically aliased periods shown in the table below:","","Table 11-1:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.4804}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2607}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2589}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Aliased Harmonic","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Frequency (1/y)","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Period (y)","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","Y/27.212-10 & 3.423 & 0.292 \\\\","Y/27.212-11 & 2.423 & 0.413 \\\\","Y/27.212-12 & 1.423 & 0.703 \\\\","Y/27.212-13 & 0.423 & 2.363 \\\\","Y/27.212-14 & -0.577 & -1.734 \\\\","Y/27.212-15 & -1.577 & -0.634 \\\\","Y/27.212-16 & -2.577 & -0.388 \\\\","\\end{longtable}","","Note that the period 2.363 years corresponding to the","4\\textsuperscript{th} entry corresponds to approximately 28 months.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-1 on page 317} is a multiple linear regression fit of","the terms from the above table using a very short training interval,","showing good cross-validation to other parts of the periodic QBO","waveform. Thus, the naturally resonant or chaotic solution is secondary","to this larger forced response {[}22{]}, {[}23{]}.","","If the lunar potential time series is varied from its stationary set of","parameters, the fit degrades rapidly. So it becomes a form of binary","match. If the model did not provide a good fit, then the lunar","gravitational potential hypothesis (as suggested by Lindzen) would be","invalidated. But since it does fit precisely when modulated and","physically aliased by a seasonal cycle, then it remains a highly","plausible and parsimonious model, and potentially useful for","understanding climate variability.","","Four key premises allow this model to be derived and then efficiently","fitted.","","\\begin{enumerate}","\\def\\labelenumi{\\arabic{enumi}.}","\\item","  The Coriolis effect cancels at the equator and we can use a small","  angle (in latitude) approximation to capture any differential effect.","\\item","  Identification of wind acceleration and not wind speed as the measure","  of QBO.","\\item","  Associating a latitudinal displacement in the equatorial line with a","  tidal elevation via a partial derivative expansion to eliminate an","  otherwise indeterminate parameter in Laplace's Tidal Equations.","\\item","  Applying a seasonal aliasing to the lunar tractive forces approximated","  by the monthly draconic/nodal cycle which ends up perfectly matching","  the observed QBO period.","\\end{enumerate}","","As the QBO has long been associated with ENSO {[}24{]}, {[}25{]}, we","will later show in more detail how similar lunar and solar tidal forces","stimulate that oceanic phenomenon.","","\\hypertarget{stochastic-wind}{%","\\subsubsection*{Stochastic Wind}\\label{stochastic-wind}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Stochastic Wind}","","Relying on wind energy as a future energy source brings up the notion of","predictable unpredictability. We realize that wind does not blow all the","time, yet that consumers demand for energy (primarily electrical)","remains constant over time. Unless we can reduce that variability,","through energy storage or geographic distribution, wind will have that","mark against it. This section explains with some very elementary","considerations of entropy why wind will remain a random phenomenon that","we will have to learn to deal with {[}26{]}, {[}27{]}. Its enduring","persistence makes it an unending source of energy if we can predictably","harness it.","","\\textbf{Wind Dispersion, the Renewable Hubbert Curve}","","Most critics of wind energy points to the unpredictability of sustained","wind speeds as a potential liability in widespread use of wind turbines.","Everyone can intuitively understand the logic behind this statement as","they have personally experienced the variability in day-to-day wind","speeds.","","However comfortably we coexist with the concept of ``windiness'', people","don't seem to understand the mathematical simplicity behind the wind","speed variability. The complexity of the earth's climate and environment","contributes to this simplicity as it generates more states for the","system to exist within, which increase the likelihood of","variability\\footnote{Wind variability also could prove useful to","  understand the dispersion of airborne particulates, such as what","  occurred in the aftermath of the 2010 Icelandic volcano.}.","","The derivation of wind dispersion starts with the premise that every","location on Earth has a mean or average wind speed. This speed has a","prevailing direction but assume that it can blow in any direction.","","Next, we safely assume that the kinetic energy contained in the","aggregate speed goes as the square of its velocity.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[E\\ \\sim\\ \\nu^{2}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-6","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This comes about from the Newtonian kinetic energy law","½\\emph{mv}\\textsuperscript{2} and it shows up empirically as the","aeronautical drag law (i.e. wind resistance) which also goes as the","square of the speed. (Note that we can consider \\emph{E} as an energy or","modified slightly as a power, since the energy is sustained over time)","","Applying the Principle of Maximum Entropy to the possible states of","energy that exist leads to a probability density function (PDF) that has","no constraints other than a mean value (with negative speeds forbidden).","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p(E) = k \\bullet e^{- kE}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-7","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{k} is a constant and 1/\\emph{k} defines the mean energy.","This describes a declining probability profile, with low energies much","more probable than high energies. To convert to a wind dispersion PDF we","substitute velocity for energy and simplify:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{matrix}","p(\\nu)\\ d\\nu = p(E)\\ dE \\\\","p(\\nu) = p(E) \\bullet \\frac{dE}{d\\nu} = 2c\\nu \\bullet e^{- c\\nu^{2}}","\\end{matrix}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-8","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This gives the empirically observed wind speed distribution, showing a","peak away from zero wind speeds and a rapid decline of frequency at","higher velocity. This is another variation of an entropic velocity","distribution, often referred to as a Rayleigh or Weibull distribution.","The excellent agreement between the model and empirical data occurs","frequently\\footnote{Wiki entry for Wind Power states: ``The Weibull","  model closely mirrors the actual distribution of hourly wind speeds at","  many locations. The Weibull factor is often close to 2 and therefore a","  Rayleigh distribution can be used as a less accurate, but simpler","  model.'', showing the distribution of wind speed and energy for all of","  2002 at the Lee Ranch facility in Colorado. The histogram shows","  measured data, while the curve is the Rayleigh model distribution for","  the same average wind speed.}.","","Many engineers use the heuristic family of Weibull curves. The Rayleigh","comes out as the simpler model because it derives from first principles","and any deviation from the quadratic exponent works as a refinement.","When using a Weibull instead of a Rayleigh\\footnote{Weibull distribution","  includes the Rayleigh as a subclass. The Weibull allows a","  parameterized exponent while Rayleigh it is set to 2. In this Weibull","  case 1.95 lies arbitrarily close to 2}, the value of 1.95 works for","all practical purposes the same as 2.","","Contrary to other distributions, the wind PDF does not qualify as a","fat-tail distribution. This becomes obvious if you consider that the","power-law only comes about from the reciprocal measure of time, and","since we measure speed directly, we invoke the entropic velocity profile","directly as well.","","So, the interesting measure relates to the indirect way that we perceive","the variations in wind speed. Only over the span of time do we detect","the unpredictability and disorder in speed --- whether by gustiness or","long periods of calm.","","We can then pose all sorts of questions based on the entropic wind speed","law. For example, how long would we have to wait to generate an","accumulated amount of energy?","","We can answer this analytically by simply equating the steady-state wind","speed to a power and then integrating over all possibilities of the","distribution that meet the minimum accumulated energy condition over a","specific time duration.","","The naive answer is trivial with the time PDF turning into the following","fat-tail power-law:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p\\left( t \\middle| E > c\\nu^{2} \\right) = c\\nu^{2} \\bullet \\frac{e^{- c\\nu^{2}}}{t^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-9","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This equation corresponds to the graphed probability density function in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-2 on page 317} (where we set an arbitrary \\emph{E} of","\\emph{cv} \\textsuperscript{2} = 25). It basically illustrates the","likelihood of accumulating a specific energy goal within a given","\\emph{t}=time.","","Because of the scarcity of high wind speeds and the finite time it takes","to accumulate energy, one observes a short ramp-up to the peak. Typical","wind-speeds round out the peak and the relatively common slow speeds","contribute to the long fat-tail.","","But since power has an extra velocity factor (\\emph{Power} =","\\emph{Drag*velocity}, see Betz\\textquotesingle{} law), it takes longer","to integrate the low power values and the exponent changes from a","quadratic value of 2 to a value of 5/3, via a chain rule.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p(t) = \\frac{2}{3} \\bullet d \\bullet \\frac{e^{- \\frac{d}{t^{\\frac{2}{3}}}}}{t^{\\frac{5}{3}}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-10","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","As one can see in the graph in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-3 on page 317}, the","fat-tail becomes fatter and the ramp-up slightly quicker for roughly the","same peak value.","","\\hypertarget{wind-energy-dispersion-analysis}{%","\\paragraph*{Wind Energy Dispersion","Analysis}\\label{wind-energy-dispersion-analysis}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Wind Energy Dispersion Analysis}","","The previous maximum entropy derivation assumed only a known mean of","wind energy levels (measured as power integrated over a fixed time","period). From this simple formulation, one can obtain a wind speed","probability graph. Knowing the probability of wind speed, you can","perform all kinds of interesting extrapolations --- for the prior","example, we can project the likelihood of how long it would take to","accumulate a certain level of energy.","","One can find abundant data on wind speed distribution in power coop","archives. One data set consisted of about 36,000 sequential hourly","measurements in terms of energy (kilowatt-hours) for Ontario. The chart","in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-4 on page 317} shows the cumulative probability","distribution function of the energy values. This shows the classic","damped exponential function, which derives from either the Maximum","Entropy Principle (probability) or the Gibbs-Boltzmann distribution","(statistics). It also shows a knee in the curve at about 750 KW-h, which","comes from a regulating governor of some sort designed to prevent the","wind turbine from damaging itself at high winds.\\footnote{Gustiness","  contributes to energy losses as the inertia of the blades can't adjust","  quickly enough to account for increases in energy. This may balance","  out the further gains we should have according to the velocity flow","  argument}","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-5 on page 317} charts the region around zero energy","to see any effect in the air flow transfer regime (which should be","strong near zero). In this regime, the probability would go as","\\(\\sqrt{E}{\\bullet e}^{- E/E_{0}}\\) instead of \\(e^{- E/E_{0}}\\).","","Yet only a linearized trend appears courtesy of the Taylor's series","expansion of the exponential around \\emph{E}=0. Remember that this data","consists of a large set of independent turbines. You might think that","because of the law of large numbers that the distribution might narrow","or show a peak. Instead, the mixture of these turbines over a wide","variation in the wind speed provides a sufficiently disordered path so","that we can apply the maximum entropy principle.","","With a gained confidence in the entropic dispersive model, we can test","the prior nagging question behind wind energy --- How long do we have to","wait until we obtain a desired level of energy?","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-6 on page 317} shows a resampled set of the data","(only resampled in the sense in that it used a wraparound at the 4-year","length of the data to create a set free from any boundary effects). The","output of the resampling essentially generated a histogram of years it","would take to reach a given energy level, at two levels,","\\emph{E(T)=}1000 MW-hrs and \\emph{E(T)=}200 MW-hrs. Plotted are the","results along with the predetermined model fit next to the data.","","The model described previously predicts the behavior applied to the two","sets of data:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p\\left( t \\middle| E > E(T) \\right) = T \\bullet \\frac{e^{- \\frac{T}{t}}}{t^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-11","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{T} is the average time it will take to reach \\emph{E(T).}","From the exponential fit in the first figure, this gives \\emph{T}=","200/178 and \\emph{T}=1000/178, respectively, for the two charts. As","expected we obtain the fat-tails that fall off as","1/\\emph{t\\textsuperscript{2}} (not 1/\\emph{t}\\textsuperscript{1.5} as","the velocity flow argument would support).","","The models do not work real effectively at the boundary conditions,","simply because the wind turbine limiting governors prevent the","accumulation of any energy levels above 1000 MWh level; this occurs","either in a short amount of time or at long times as a Poisson process","of multiple gusts of lower energy. That said, any real deviations likely","arise from short-duration correlations between wind energy measurements","spaced close together. We do see this as the lower limit of E(200) shows","more correlation curvature than E(1000) does. Wind speeds do change","gradually so these correlations will occur; yet these seem minor","perturbations on the fundamental entropic dispersion model, which seems","to work quite well under these conditions.","","\\textbf{Germany}","","As a bottom-line, this analysis tells us what we already intuited.","Because of intermittency in wind speed, it often takes a long time to","accumulate a specific level of energy. Everyone knows this from their","day-to-day experience dealing with the elements. However, the principle","of maximum entropy allows us to draw on some rather simple probability","formula so that we can make some additional estimates for long-term use.","","By adding more data to our knowledge on wind dispersion, we can observe","how dispersion in wind speeds has a universal character. The set in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-7 on page 317} is collected from northwest Germany","and consists of wind power collected at 15-minute intervals.","","Note that the same entropic dispersion holds as for Ontario. Both curves","display the same damped exponential probability distribution function","for frequency of wind power (derived from wind speed). We also see the","same qualitative cut-out above a certain power or wind energy level.","Once again, note that we don't gain much by drawing from these higher","power levels as they occur more sporadically than the nominally rated","wind speeds at the upper reaches of the curve, and which may cut-out","above a ``max'' wind-speed.\\footnote{A typical turbine specification","  will regulate power with a cut-in wind speed: 3 m/s, rated wind speed:","  12 m/s, and cut-out wind speed: 25 m/s}","","The sporadic nature of wind confronts us with an unavoidable issue of","consistent power output {[}29{]}. The entropic dispersion of wind acts","much like a variation of the Carnot cycle. In the Carnot cycle of engine","efficiency, we must live with a maximum level of energy conversion based","on temperature differences of the input and output reservoirs. With","wind, the earth's environment and atmosphere provides the","temperature-like differences which leads directly to the variability","over time.","","So, we can easily imagine achieving very high usage efficiency if we","acknowledge the entropic characteristics of the wind. This amounts to a","law of nature. We need to talk about efficiencies within the constraints","of the physical laws just as with the Carnot cycle. We will observe","intermittency because of entropic dispersion and we must become","accustomed to it. We should not call it a fundamental ``problem'', as we","cannot change the characteristics of entropy (apart from adding energy,","and that just moves us back to square one).","","Consider this from a different prospective: certain people would suggest","that the fundamental problem with farming derives from the intermittent","nature of the rain\\footnote{It has been said that maintaining human","  civilization is dependent on 6 inches of topsoil and the fact that it","  occasionally rains.}. With farming, we adapt --- likewise with wind","energy. Instead of a problem, we need to call it an opportunity and deal","with the predictable unpredictability. The derivation essentially","becomes the equivalent of a permanent weather forecast. We should","realize that the wind speed will eventually pick up and not to fret too","much about it\\footnote{Again, both dispersive discovery and the entropic","  wind dispersion model use the same set of ideas from probability. The","  analysis can see through the complexity and discover the underlying","  elegance and intuitive power of simple entropy arguments, one a","  non-renewable resource and the second renewable.}.","","If that instills too much uncertainty, we should also consider that","electrical power as it stands right now comes from distributed sources.","Considering that wind shows variability, distributing power from","turbines in geographically separated areas will help regulate the flow,","as different regions will ebb and flow in wind speed. This is part of","the smart grid concept {[}30{]}, {[}31{]}.","","\\hypertarget{alternate-derivation-and-super-statistics}{%","\\paragraph*{Alternate derivation and super","statistics}\\label{alternate-derivation-and-super-statistics}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Alternate derivation and super","statistics}","","Wind velocities demonstrate a wide dynamic variability, ranging from","calm to gale force. With minimal knowledge as to the origin of the wind","variance, we can apply the maximum entropy principle to its energy","content. The derivation of wind dispersion follows a few straightforward","steps. Next we safely assume that the kinetic energy contained in the","aggregate speed goes as the square of its velocity. If we assume only","temporally-averaged mean wind energy and then relate the energy,","\\emph{\\textbf{E}}, as the square of the wind speed,","\\textbf{\\emph{ν}\\textsuperscript{2}}, the resultant maximum entropy","probability distribution matches the Rayleigh distribution.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p(v) = p(E) \\bullet \\frac{dE}{dv} = 2cv \\bullet e^{- cv^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-12","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This comes about from the Newtonian kinetic energy law","½\\emph{mv}\\textsuperscript{2} and it shows up empirically as the","aeronautical drag law (i.e. wind resistance) which also goes as the","square of the speed.\\footnote{Note that we can consider \\emph{E} as an","  energy or modified slightly as a power, since the energy is sustained","  over time} Then apply the principle of maximum entropy to the possible","states of energy that exist and come up with a probability density","function that has no constraints other than a mean value (with negative","speeds forbidden). In the equation above \\emph{c} is a constant and","1/\\emph{c} defines the mean energy (i.e. essentially acting as the","Boltzmann activation energy). This describes a declining probability","profile, with low energies much more probable than high energies. To","convert to a wind dispersion PDF we substitute velocity for energy and","simplify.","","FIG.11-4 shows an empirically observed wind speed distribution, showing","a peak away from zero wind speeds and a rapid decline of frequency at","higher velocity. Heuristically, many scientists refer to the model as","following a Rayleigh or Weibull distribution. The Rayleigh comes out as","the simpler model because it derives from first principles and any","deviation from the quadratic exponent works as a refinement. The first","data set shown consisted of about 36,000 sequential hourly measurements","in terms of energy (kilowatt-hours) for Ontario.","","By adding more data to our knowledge on wind dispersion, we can observe","how dispersion in wind speeds has a universal character. The second data","set (FIG.11-7) comes from northwest Germany and consists of wind power","collected at 15 minute intervals.","","Note that the same wind dispersion holds as for Ontario. Both curves","display the same damped exponential probability distribution function","for frequency of wind power (derived from wind speed). We also see the","same qualitative cut-out above a certain power or wind energy level.","","\\textbf{Adding More Variability.} Since \\emph{E\\textsubscript{A}} can","vary from region to region, we leave it as a conditional, and then set","that as a maximal entropy estimator as well","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p\\left( E_{i} \\right) = \\ \\alpha \\bullet e^{- \\alpha E_{i}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-13","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","then we integrate over the conditional\\textquotesingle s range according","to standard practice and arrive at a cumulative.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P(E) = \\int_{0}^{\\infty}{P\\left( E \\middle| E_{i} \\right)p\\left( E_{i} \\right)dE_{i}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-14","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This results in a simple lookup in a comprehensive table of cataloged","integration formulas, which leads to the following solution:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P(E) = 2\\ \\sqrt{\\frac{E}{\\overline{E}}}\\ K_{1}\\left( 2\\ \\sqrt{\\frac{E}{\\overline{E}}} \\right)\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-15","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{K\\textsubscript{1}} is the modified BesselK function of the","second kind, in this case of order 1, which is found in any spreadsheet","program (such as Excel). Note that this is the same function that we","used for the distribution of terrain slopes.~ The order 1 is the variant","used for the \\emph{cumulative} distribution function\\\\","\\strut \\\\","We tested this formulation against wind data from Bonneville Power","Administration, which has over 20 meteorological stations set up around","northern Oregon. The download consisted of over 2.5 million data points","collected at 5 minute intervals, archived over the span of a little less","than 2 years.","","For the fit of this curve in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-8 on page 317}, the","average energy was derived directly by computing the mean over the","entire set of data separately. This corresponded to a value of 12.4 MPH,","and placed a pair of positive and negative tolerances to give an idea of","the sensitivity of the fit.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","As this is a single parameter model, the only leeway we have is in","shifting the curve horizontally along the energy axis, and since this is","locked by an average, the fit becomes almost automatic with no room for","tweaking and little for argument. The probabilities are automatically","normalized.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-9 on page 317} shows the log-log plot, which reveals","a departure at high wind speeds. This shows that excessive gale force","winds (greater than 60 MPH) did not occur over the extended region","during the span of two years data collection.~","","Wind dispersion analysis has obvious applications for context modeling.","Fuel efficiency is impacted by aerodynamics and drag goes up as the","square of the wind speed. Vehicle cooling also is impacted by convection","due to local winds. Applying this approach for context modeling has the","benefit of allowing simple sampled data generation for verification and","PCC bounding, similar to that applied for terrain slopes.","","The previous maximum entropy derivation assumed only a known mean of","wind energy levels (measured as power integrated over a fixed time","period). From this simple formulation, one can estimate or extrapolate a","wind speed probability. Knowing the probability of wind speed, one can","also perform all kinds of interesting extrapolations --- for example, we","can project the likelihood of how long it would take to accumulate a","certain level of energy{[}32{]}.","","\\hypertarget{spatial-and-temporal-correlations-of-wind}{%","\\subsubsection*{\\texorpdfstring{Spatial and Temporal Correlations of","Wind","}{Spatial and Temporal Correlations of Wind }}\\label{spatial-and-temporal-correlations-of-wind}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Spatial and Temporal Correlations","of Wind }","","In terms of climate statistics, we always have more data than we know","what to do with. The challenge is in reducing the data into meaningful","bits of information which can be characterized and modeled succinctly.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","So this short note describes an example of where enough data exists that","we can obtain a deeper understanding of climate phenomena; in this case","of the spatial and temporal correlations of prevailing winds.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","One source of data comes from measurements of winds at altitude, taken","by jet airliners as part of their regular flight routes and then","post-processed and analyzed {[}33{]}. This gives an indication of","spatial correlation over a large dynamic range, stretching a little over","three orders of magnitude.~ Once collected, the post-processing involved","applying an autocorrelation to the data over all spatial scales.~ The","power spectral density (PSD) is the Fourier transform of the","autocorrelation, and that is shown in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-10 on page 317}","below, along with a suggested model achieving a good fit to the actual","meridonal wind PSD.","","The chosen model is simply the PSD of an exponentially damped cosine","autocorrelation (see {[}34{]} for examples in meteorology).","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[C(x) = e^{- \\alpha|x|}\\ cos(\\beta x)\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-16","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","The Fourier transform of this autocorrelation gives a PSD that is best","described as a shifted Cauchy or Lorentzian profile.~ In","\\textbf{FIG.11-10}, the shape is obvious as it shows a peak and then a","concave-upward inflection following the peak.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[I\\left( S_{x} \\right) = \\frac{\\alpha}{\\left( S_{x} - \\beta \\right)^{2} + \\alpha^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","11-17","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This specific profile is generated by a semi-Markovian process.~ The","\"semi\" part captures the periodic portion while the Markov contributes","the memory for close-proximity coupling. Specifically, the Markov term","is the alpha-factor damping which gives the overall","1/S\\textsuperscript{2} roll-off, while the beta-spatial shift indicates","a semi-Markov ordering feature indicating some underlying longer-range","spatial periodicity.","","\\begin{itemize}","\\item","  \\emph{β} =2\\emph{π}/\\emph{L}=1.3E-6 rad/m","\\item","  \\emph{α}~=7.63E-7 rad/m","\\end{itemize}","","This gives a weak periodicity of L=4833 km.~~ For a prevailing","wind-stream of 30 MPH, this spans approximately 4 days of flow between a","peak and a lull in wind speed.~\\\\","\\strut \\\\","The spatial correlation intuitively leads to the concept of a temporal","correlation in wind speed, and the question of whether this","characteristic can be measured and contrasted to the spatial","correlation.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","For temporal correlation, we used the same data from the BPA which we","had previously applied to a probability density function (PDF)","analysis.~","","The temporal autocorrelation of the Roosevelt Island site","(\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-11 on page 317}) shows some periodic fine structure.","Using the Eureqa equation fitting software, we obtain the following","empirical autocorrelation match.","","\\emph{c}(\\emph{t})=0.02934⋅\\emph{sin}(0.5012−1.626\\emph{t})+0.01668⋅~\\emph{cos}(0.8645+6.203\\emph{t})+\\emph{exp}(−1.405\\emph{t})−0.02134⋅\\emph{sin}(1.088−0.599\\emph{t})","\\textbf{11-18}","","The first and second terms are periodicities of 3.86 days and","\\textasciitilde1 day, the latter easily explained by daily (diurnal)","variations.~ There is also a strong damping factor with a half-life less","than a day. The last term is a 10 day period which generates a longer","term modulation (and also has more uncertainty in its weighting).\\\\","\\strut \\\\","The 3.86 day periodicity is likely due to a principle oscillation","pattern in unstable meandering baroclinic Rossby waves as detected","through a separate statistical analysis{[}35{]}.","","This demonstrates how the spatial correlation relates to the temporal","correlation. As the tropospheric wave patterns propagate and disperse,","they influence the local wind patterns.~ These are quite subtle effects","yet they can be detected and perhaps can be put to good use in battling","the intermittency in wind power.","","\\textbf{\\hfill\\break","}","","\\hypertarget{list-of-figure-captions}{%","\\section*{List of Figure Captions}\\label{list-of-figure-captions}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{List of Figure Captions}","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-1}:","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-1.png}{} Model","match to QBO 70 hPa data","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-2}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-2.png}{} A","derived distribution from the data set which plots time it takes to","accumulate a set energy level in the context of a variable wind source.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-3}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-3.png}{} A","variation of the accumulated energy whereby an added drag term will","reduce the efficiency and therefore the likelihood of accumulating","energy quickly.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-4}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-4.png}{}","Probability distribution function of wind speeds in Ontario, Canada.","This follows the Rayleigh distribution according to the straight line","fit. Deviation from this curve at higher wind speeds results from an","automated cut-out of the generator to prevent damage to the turbine or","blades. Although these will provide high energy density, their rarity","prevents them from contributing much to the cumulative.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-5}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-5.png}{} Blow-up","of previous figure showing slight divergence at smaller wind energies.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-6}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-6.png}{} (a) (b)","Derived distributions for time to reach a certain energy level. The","longer it takes to reach a certain level, the greater the likelihood","that the curve will match the predicted levels.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-7}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-7.png}{} Wind","variations for Germany. The curve has all the same characteristics as","that for Ontario, demonstrating the universal behavior in wind speed","variability.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-8}.","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-8.png}{}","Cumulative distribution function of wind energies from Bonneville with","model fit","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-9}:","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-9.png}{}","Cumulative distribution function of wind energies on a log-log plot.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_11-10}:","\\href{../figures/FIGURE_11-10.png}{} Zonal","and Meridional wind speed PSD.
